
======================================================
VLM frame 0 (window 0-0)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

==============================================================

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Downtown Market (grocery)
Description: Neighborhood store with fresh produce and daily essentials.
Location: 123 King St W, Toronto
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: medium
Allowed behaviors: Employees carry boxes, Customers stay in store for long periods, People smoke near entrance
Forbidden behaviors: holding a gun, holding a knife
Surveillance cameras (2) — all video frames analyzed are captured from these cameras:
  - Front Entrance Cam: Mounted above the main entrance door
  - Checkout Cam: Overlooking all cash registers

NOW — current window (oldest → most recent; compare against EARLIER):
- (frame 0) VLM observation: Two individuals are inside a gun store, with one person leaning over a glass display case while the other walks through the center of the room. The person at the display case appears to be reaching into or interacting with the contents of the case. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 2 persons.

Respond with exactly one JSON object and nothing else.

RAW OUTPUT FROM GROQ (narrative only — score/label are code-computed):
{
  "reason": "Individual interacts with display case, potential escalation",
  "key_moments": [
    "person leans over glass display case",
    "another person walks through the center of the room",
    "reaching into the display case"
  ],
  "concern": "medium"
}

FINAL RESULT (code-scored):
{
  "anomaly_score": 0.08,
  "label": "normal",
  "reason": "Individual interacts with display case, potential escalation",
  "key_moments": [
    "person leans over glass display case",
    "another person walks through the center of the room",
    "reaching into the display case"
  ]
}

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Downtown Market (grocery)
Description: Neighborhood store with fresh produce and daily essentials.
Location: 123 King St W, Toronto
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: medium
Allowed behaviors: Employees carry boxes, Customers stay in store for long periods, People smoke near entrance
Forbidden behaviors: holding a gun, holding a knife
Surveillance cameras (2) — all video frames analyzed are captured from these cameras:
  - Front Entrance Cam: Mounted above the main entrance door
  - Checkout Cam: Overlooking all cash registers
EARLIER observations (oldest → most recent):
- (frame 0) VLM observation: Two individuals are inside a gun store, with one person leaning over a glass display case while the other walks through the center of the room. The person at the display case appears to be reaching into or interacting with the contents of the case. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 2 persons.

NOW — current window (oldest → most recent; compare against EARLIER):
- (frame 0) VLM observation: Two individuals are inside a gun store, with one person leaning over a glass display case while the other walks through the center of the room. The person at the display case appears to be reaching into or interacting with the contents of the case. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 2 persons.
- (frame 60) VLM observation: Two individuals dressed in dark clothing are inside a retail store, with one person leaning over a display case while the other stands nearby. The scene shows signs of a potential burglary or robbery in progress. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes.

Respond with exactly one JSON object and nothing else.

RAW OUTPUT FROM GROQ (narrative only — score/label are code-computed):
{
  "reason": "Scene escalates with face concealment and potential robbery",
  "key_moments": [
    "subject leans over display case, reaching in",
    "face concealment detected, increasing suspicious behavior",
    "second individual stands nearby, potential accomplice"
  ],
  "concern": "high"
}

FINAL RESULT (code-scored):
{
  "anomaly_score": 0.2,
  "label": "normal",
  "reason": "Scene escalates with face concealment and potential robbery",
  "key_moments": [
    "subject leans over display case, reaching in",
    "face concealment detected, increasing suspicious behavior",
    "second individual stands nearby, potential accomplice"
  ]
}

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Downtown Market (grocery)
Description: Neighborhood store with fresh produce and daily essentials.
Location: 123 King St W, Toronto
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: medium
Allowed behaviors: Employees carry boxes, Customers stay in store for long periods, People smoke near entrance
Forbidden behaviors: holding a gun, holding a knife
Surveillance cameras (2) — all video frames analyzed are captured from these cameras:
  - Front Entrance Cam: Mounted above the main entrance door
  - Checkout Cam: Overlooking all cash registers
EARLIER observations (oldest → most recent):
- (frame 0) VLM observation: Two individuals are inside a gun store, with one person leaning over a glass display case while the other walks through the center of the room. The person at the display case appears to be reaching into or interacting with the contents of the case. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 2 persons.
- (frame 60) VLM observation: Two individuals dressed in dark clothing are inside a retail store, with one person leaning over a display case while the other stands nearby. The scene shows signs of a potential burglary or robbery in progress. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes.

NOW — current window (oldest → most recent; compare against EARLIER):
- (frame 0) VLM observation: Two individuals are inside a gun store, with one person leaning over a glass display case while the other walks through the center of the room. The person at the display case appears to be reaching into or interacting with the contents of the case. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 2 persons.
- (frame 60) VLM observation: Two individuals dressed in dark clothing are inside a retail store, with one person leaning over a display case while the other stands nearby. The scene shows signs of a potential burglary or robbery in progress. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes.
- (frame 120) VLM observation: Two individuals are leaning over and reaching into a glass display case inside a retail store. Both individuals are dressed in dark, hooded clothing. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 1 person.

Respond with exactly one JSON object and nothing else.

RAW OUTPUT FROM GROQ (narrative only — score/label are code-computed):
{
  "reason": "Scene escalates with two individuals reaching into display case",
  "key_moments": [
    "two individuals lean over display case",
    "both reach into the case",
    "one person's face concealment status changes to unclear"
  ],
  "concern": "high"
}

FINAL RESULT (code-scored):
{
  "anomaly_score": 0.13,
  "label": "normal",
  "reason": "Scene escalates with two individuals reaching into display case",
  "key_moments": [
    "two individuals lean over display case",
    "both reach into the case",
    "one person's face concealment status changes to unclear"
  ]
}

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Downtown Market (grocery)
Description: Neighborhood store with fresh produce and daily essentials.
Location: 123 King St W, Toronto
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: medium
Allowed behaviors: Employees carry boxes, Customers stay in store for long periods, People smoke near entrance
Forbidden behaviors: holding a gun, holding a knife
Surveillance cameras (2) — all video frames analyzed are captured from these cameras:
  - Front Entrance Cam: Mounted above the main entrance door
  - Checkout Cam: Overlooking all cash registers

NOW — current window (oldest → most recent; compare against EARLIER):
- (frame 120) VLM observation: Two individuals are leaning over and reaching into a glass display case inside a retail store. Both individuals are dressed in dark, hooded clothing. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 180) VLM observation: An individual is standing behind the counter in a gun store, appearing to handle items from the display case. Another person is positioned behind the counter near a computer monitor. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 240) VLM observation: An individual is standing in the center of a gun store, appearing to handle or inspect an object held in their hands. The store displays numerous firearms on the walls and within glass counters. Weapon note: The person is holding an object that appears to be a firearm.. Flags — gun: unclear. Tracker detected: 1 person.

Respond with exactly one JSON object and nothing else.

RAW OUTPUT FROM GROQ (narrative only — score/label are code-computed):
{
  "reason": "Individual handling object resembling firearm in store",
  "key_moments": [
    "subject reaches into display case",
    "person stands behind counter, handling items",
    "individual inspects object in hands, potentially a firearm"
  ],
  "concern": "high"
}

FINAL RESULT (code-scored):
{
  "anomaly_score": 0.7,
  "label": "suspicious",
  "reason": "Individual handling object resembling firearm in store",
  "key_moments": [
    "subject reaches into display case",
    "person stands behind counter, handling items",
    "individual inspects object in hands, potentially a firearm"
  ]
}

headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

VLM frame 360 (window 240-360)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural

escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.

context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

- Store: Downtown Market (grocery)

  - Front Entrance Cam: Mounted above the main entrance door
  - Checkout Cam: Overlooking all cash registers

Tracker detected: 1 person.

The individual leaning over the display case appears to be reaching into or interacting with the contents of the case. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: unclear; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 360) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively smashing and reaching into glass display cases inside a gun store. Both individuals are dressed in dark clothing and appear to be looting merchandise from the cases. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes; aggression: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.

Both individuals are dressed in dark, hooded clothing that obscures their identities. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 660) VLM observation: Two individuals are leaning over and reaching into glass display cases inside a retail store, appearing to be in the process of removing merchandise. Both individuals are dressed in dark, full-coverage clothing and appear to be concealing their faces. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.

NOW — current window (oldest → most recent; compare against EARLIER):
- (frame 600) VLM observation: Two individuals dressed in dark clothing are leaning over and reaching into the glass display cases of a gun store. The individuals appear to be actively removing or searching through the merchandise inside the cases. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.

Both individuals are dressed in dark, full-coverage clothing and appear to be concealing their faces. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 720) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively looting a display case inside a gun store, with one person reaching into the case while the other stands nearby. The store walls are lined with numerous firearms on display. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.

INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in

from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving

======================================================
VLM frame 1020 (window 900-1020)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

======================================================
VLM frame 1200 (window 1080-1200)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

======================================================
VLM frame 1380 (window 1260-1380)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

======================================================
VLM frame 1560 (window 1440-1560)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

======================================================
VLM frame 1740 (window 1620-1740)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

==============================================================

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Downtown Market (grocery)
Description: Neighborhood store with fresh produce and daily essentials.
Location: 123 King St W, Toronto
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: medium
Allowed behaviors: Employees carry boxes, Customers stay in store for long periods, People smoke near entrance
Forbidden behaviors: holding a gun, holding a knife
Surveillance cameras (2) — all video frames analyzed are captured from these cameras:
  - Front Entrance Cam: Mounted above the main entrance door
  - Checkout Cam: Overlooking all cash registers
EARLIER observations (oldest → most recent):
- (frame 1380) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively looting a gun store, with one person pulling firearms from a wall display while the other reaches into a shattered glass display case. Both individuals are dressed in dark, full-body clothing and appear to be concealing their identities. Weapon note: Multiple firearms are visible being handled and removed from the wall display.. Flags — gun: yes; reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes; aggression: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 1440) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively looting a gun store, with one person pulling firearms from a wall display while the other gathers items from a display case. Both individuals are wearing dark clothing and hoods, and they appear to be in the process of stealing merchandise. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes; aggression: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 1500) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively looting a gun store, with one person reaching into a shattered display case while the other is positioned behind the counter. Both individuals are dressed in dark, full-body clothing and appear to be concealing their identities. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: yes; face concealed: yes; aggression: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 1560) VLM observation: Two individuals are inside a gun store, with one person leaning over a display case while the other stands near the counter. The person leaning over the case appears to be reaching into or interacting with the contents of the display. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 1620) VLM observation: Two individuals dressed in dark clothing are actively looting a gun store, with one person leaning over a display case while the other stands nearby. The store's display cases appear to have been shattered, and merchandise is being removed. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes; aggression: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 1680) VLM observation: Two individuals are standing near a shattered glass display case in the center of a gun store. The individuals appear to be in the process of removing items from the broken display. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 1740) VLM observation: An individual is standing behind the counter in a retail store, appearing to be in the process of removing items from the display wall. The store is empty of other people. Weapon note: Multiple firearms are visible on the wall display behind the individual.. Flags — gun: yes; reaching behind counter: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 1800) VLM observation: An individual is standing behind a counter in a store, appearing to reach toward items on the shelves. The scene is captured in low-light conditions with a text overlay indicating an alternate view of a commercial burglary. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 1860) VLM observation: An individual is standing behind the counter in a store, while another person is positioned in front of the counter, appearing to interact with the display. Flags — reaching display case: yes.

NOW — current window (oldest → most recent; compare against EARLIER):
- (frame 1800) VLM observation: An individual is standing behind a counter in a store, appearing to reach toward items on the shelves. The scene is captured in low-light conditions with a text overlay indicating an alternate view of a commercial burglary. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 1860) VLM observation: An individual is standing behind the counter in a store, while another person is positioned in front of the counter, appearing to interact with the display. Flags — reaching display case: yes.
- (frame 1920) VLM observation: An individual is standing behind the counter in a gun store, while a customer is positioned in front of the counter browsing merchandise. Flags — none.

Respond with exactly one JSON object and nothing else.

RAW OUTPUT FROM GROQ (narrative only — score/label are code-computed):
{
  "reason": "Scene resolves as customer browses merchandise",
  "key_moments": [
    "Individual steps back from counter",
    "Customer appears in front of counter, browsing",
    "No aggressive behavior or weapons visible"
  ],
  "concern": "low"
}

FINAL RESULT (code-scored):
{
  "anomaly_score": 0.3,
  "label": "suspicious",
  "reason": "Scene resolves as customer browses merchandise",
  "key_moments": [
    "Individual steps back from counter",
    "Customer appears in front of counter, browsing",
    "No aggressive behavior or weapons visible"
  ]
}

lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it

======================================================
VLM frame 2340 (window 2220-2340)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."

======================================================
VLM frame 2580 (window 2460-2580)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

======================================================
VLM frame 3000 (window 2880-3000)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes
