======================================================
VLM frame 3180 (window 3060-3180)
======================================================
INPUT TO GROQ:
You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

You are a security analyst reviewing a sequence of camera observations from a store, in
chronological order. Your job is to write a short narrative of what is happening and how the
scene has changed — so a human reader instantly understands whether the situation is
escalating, stable, or resolving. You are NOT restating isolated facts, and you are NOT
deciding a numeric score or a normal/suspicious/criminal label — that is computed separately
from the structured evidence. Your only job is the narrative and your honest read of concern.

You receive:
1. Business context — this store's specific allowed and forbidden behaviors.
2. EARLIER observations — VLM (vision model) readings from recent prior decision points,
   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes

==============================================================

Here is the scene, in chronological order:

Business context (from NestJS):
- Store: Downtown Market (grocery)
Description: Neighborhood store with fresh produce and daily essentials.
Location: 123 King St W, Toronto
Sensitivity: high; scoring: aggressive; interaction: medium
Allowed behaviors: Employees carry boxes, Customers stay in store for long periods, People smoke near entrance
Forbidden behaviors: holding a gun, holding a knife
Surveillance cameras (2) — all video frames analyzed are captured from these cameras:
  - Front Entrance Cam: Mounted above the main entrance door
  - Checkout Cam: Overlooking all cash registers
EARLIER observations (oldest → most recent):
- (frame 2580) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively reaching into and handling merchandise inside open display cases in what appears to be a retail store. Both individuals are dressed in dark clothing and are positioned behind the counter area. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: yes. Tracker detected: 2 persons.
- (frame 2640) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively removing merchandise from display cases in what appears to be a burglary. One person is placing items into a large bag while the other leans over a counter to access items. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: yes; face concealed: unclear. Tracker detected: 2 persons.
- (frame 2940) VLM observation: A person is leaning over a shattered glass display case inside a store, with a yellow tool lying on the floor nearby. The individual appears to be reaching into the display case to access merchandise. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear.
- (frame 3000) VLM observation: An individual is actively smashing a glass display case with a long-handled tool inside a retail store. The person is leaning over the counter area while debris from the shattered glass is visible on the floor. Weapon note: A long-handled tool, possibly a hammer or mallet, is being used to break the glass.. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear; aggression: yes.
- (frame 3060) VLM observation: An individual is actively smashing a glass display case with a tool, likely a hammer, inside a retail store. The person is leaning over the broken glass to access the merchandise inside. Weapon note: A tool, possibly a hammer, is being used to break the glass.. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear; aggression: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.
- (frame 3120) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively engaged in a smash-and-grab burglary, with one person climbing over a display counter while the other stands nearby. The store's glass display case appears to have been shattered, with debris visible on the floor. Flags — reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: yes; face concealed: yes; aggression: yes.
- (frame 3180) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively removing merchandise from a wall display and a counter area inside a retail store. Both individuals are wearing dark clothing and face coverings while appearing to commit a theft. Weapon note: The individuals are handling multiple items from a wall display that appear to be firearms.. Flags — gun: yes; reaching display case: yes; reaching behind counter: yes; face concealed: yes; aggression: yes.
- (frame 3240) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively breaking into a glass display case inside a retail store, with one person reaching into the shattered glass while the other stands nearby. The scene shows signs of a forced entry or robbery in progress. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear; aggression: yes.
- (frame 3300) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively breaking into a glass display case in a retail store, with one person reaching inside to remove merchandise. Both individuals are dressed in dark clothing and appear to be engaged in a theft. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear; aggression: yes.

NOW — current window (oldest → most recent; compare against EARLIER):
- (frame 3240) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively breaking into a glass display case inside a retail store, with one person reaching into the shattered glass while the other stands nearby. The scene shows signs of a forced entry or robbery in progress. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear; aggression: yes.
- (frame 3300) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively breaking into a glass display case in a retail store, with one person reaching inside to remove merchandise. Both individuals are dressed in dark clothing and appear to be engaged in a theft. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: unclear; aggression: yes.
- (frame 3360) VLM observation: Two individuals are actively breaking into a glass display case inside a retail store, with one person leaning over the counter while the other stands nearby. The scene shows signs of forced entry, including shattered glass on the floor. Flags — reaching display case: yes; face concealed: yes; aggression: yes. Tracker detected: 1 person.

Respond with exactly one JSON object and nothing else.

RAW OUTPUT FROM GROQ (narrative only — score/label are code-computed):
{
  "reason": "Robbery in progress with forced entry and aggression",
  "key_moments": [
    "individuals break into glass display case",
    "one person leans over counter while the other stands nearby",
    "scene shows signs of forced entry with shattered glass on floor",
    "aggression and face concealment increase"
  ],
  "concern": "high"
}

FINAL RESULT (code-scored):
{
  "anomaly_score": 1.0,
  "label": "criminal",
  "reason": "Robbery in progress with forced entry and aggression",
  "key_moments": [
    "individuals break into glass display case",
    "one person leans over counter while the other stands nearby",
    "scene shows signs of forced entry with shattered glass on floor",
    "aggression and face concealment increase"
  ]
}

   oldest first.
3. NOW — the current window's VLM reading(s), the one(s) you are reporting on.

==================== HOW TO WRITE THE NARRATIVE ====================

### 1. Compare NOW against EARLIER — describe the trajectory, not a snapshot
Look at what changed between the earlier observations and now: movement, positioning, new
people, new flags (weapon, aggression, concealment, forbidden actions). Is a person moving
toward the counter, closing distance, escalating from browsing to something else? Say so.

### 2. Ground every claim in the evidence you were given — do not invent motion
Only describe actions, positions, or objects stated in the EARLIER/NOW lines or business
context. If nothing meaningfully changed between EARLIER and NOW, say the scene is stable
(e.g. "scene remains stable, customer still browsing the display case") — do NOT invent
escalation, movement, or a weapon that isn't supported by the text just to make the narrative
more dramatic. A stable, boring scene deserves a stable, boring narrative.

### 3. Match behavior to the business context BY SPECIFIC WORDING, NOT GENERAL TOPIC
Before writing, check which specific Allowed/Forbidden list item each described action actually
matches — do not pattern-match on a general theme (e.g. "reaching" or "counter") and jump to the
nearest-sounding Forbidden item. Reaching INTO or OVER a display case to view/handle merchandise
matches an Allowed item ("Employees opening display cases", "Customers trying on jewelry") — it
is NOT the same as reaching BEHIND the CASHIER COUNTER (the employee-only space), which is what
a Forbidden item like "Reaching behind cashier counter" actually describes.

### 4. Appearance is context only
Clothing, hoods, masks, and hats are frequently benign (fashion, weather, religious or cultural
headwear). Mention appearance only as a MODIFIER of an action you are already describing, never
as a claim on its own. A "face concealed" flag means the VLM judged the face ITSELF hidden —
plain headwear mentioned in appearance text is NOT concealment; never infer concealment yourself
from appearance words alone.

### 5. Weapons and forbidden actions are the strongest signal
If a weapon flag or forbidden-action flag appears, that is the most important thing to narrate —
lead with it. An "unclear" flag (e.g. "gun: unclear", "aggression: unclear") is weak-to-moderate
escalating evidence, worth mentioning as developing concern — do not treat it as nothing, and do
not treat it as confirmed either.

==================== OUTPUT FORMAT (STRICT) ====================
Output EXACTLY one JSON object with these keys — no extra text before or after:
- "reason": a short analyst narrative (max ~45 words) describing what is happening and how it
  has changed since the earlier observations — write it the way a human security analyst would
  describe the situation to a colleague, not a restated flag.
- "key_moments": 2-5 short clauses describing what people are doing / how the scene changed
  (e.g. "subject closes distance to the seller", "posture becomes more assertive") — never
  isolated flag names, never appearance alone, never raw tracker/technical data.
- "concern": one of "low", "medium", "high" — your own honest instinct for how concerning this
  moment is. This is advisory only: the actual anomaly score and label are computed separately
  from the structured evidence, not from this field, so answer honestly rather than trying to
  guess a target number.

Do NOT include "anomaly_score" or "label" fields in your output — they are not yours to decide.

Examples of the narrative voice we want (style only — do not reuse this wording verbatim):
- "Capped male enters and moves directly toward the counter with limited browsing behavior."
- "Subject reaches the counter area and body language becomes more assertive, though weapon
  visibility is still uncertain."
- "Handgun becomes visible near the seller, indicating the start of an armed robbery."

Examples of what NOT to write (too terse, cue-echoing — FORBIDDEN style):
- "Reaching display case"
- "Face concealed, reaching display case"
- "person wearing a hood" (appearance stated with no action)
- Any mention of tracker IDs, confidence scores, or bounding boxes
